### [1] [A GPU-Accelerated Hybrid Method for a Class of Multi-Depot Vehicle Routing Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05208)
*Zhenyu Lei,Jin-Kao Hao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a hybrid algorithm for multi-depot vehicle routing problems, integrating learning-driven crossover, multi-depot search framework, depot-specific local search, and GPU acceleration, achieving state-of-the-art results on benchmark instances.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MDVRPs are prevalent in practice but computationally challenging to solve due to their inherent complexity, motivating the need for effective hybrid algorithms.

Method: The algorithm integrates (1) a learning-driven, diversity-controlled route-exchange crossover, (2) a multi-depot-supported feasible-and-infeasible search framework guided by a multi-penalty evaluation function, (3) two dedicated depot-related local search operators, and (4) tensor-based GPU acceleration with a multi-move update strategy.

Result: Extensive experiments on three MDVRP variants show the proposed algorithms are highly competitive with state-of-the-art methods, especially for large-scale instances.

Conclusion: The hybrid algorithm with GPU acceleration is an effective and scalable approach for solving multi-depot vehicle routing problems, outperforming existing methods on large instances.

Abstract: Multi-depot vehicle routing problems (MDVRPs) are prevalent in a variety of practical applications. However, they are computationally challenging to solve due to their inherent complexity. This paper proposes an effective hybrid algorithm for a class of MDVRPs. The algorithm integrates a learning-driven, diversity-controlled route-exchange crossover and a multi-depot-supported feasible-and-infeasible search framework guided by a multi-penalty evaluation function. Two dedicated depot-related local search operators are incorporated to further strengthen the search capability in multi-depot settings. To improve computational efficiency and scalability, an enhanced version of the algorithm is developed that uses a tensor-based GPU acceleration combined with a novel multi-move update strategy. Extensive computational experiments on benchmark instances of three MDVRP variants show that the proposed algorithms are highly competitive with state-of-the-art methods, especially for large-scale instances.

</details>


### [2] [Topology-Driven Anti-Entanglement Control for Soft Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05236)
*Haoyang Le,Shengxuan Wang,Mohan Chen,Shuo Feng*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a topology-driven multi-agent reinforcement learning (TD-MARL) framework for anti-winding control of soft robots in complex constrained environments, which uses centralized learning with shared topological states and distributed execution without communication, and integrates a topological security layer to assess entanglement risk.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current distributed training frameworks face observability challenges in high-density barrier and unstable environments, resulting in poor learning outcomes for coordinating multiple robots to complete unwinding operations in highly constrained environments.

Method: TD-MARL framework: (1) Centralized learning with critical network perceiving strategies of other agents via shared topological states; (2) Distributed execution eliminating communication demands; (3) Integrated topological security layer using topological invariants to assess and mitigate entanglement risk.

Result: Simulation experiments show the proposed method outperforms current advanced deep reinforcement learning (DRL) methods in terms of convergence and anti-winding effect.

Conclusion: The TD-MARL framework effectively addresses observability and training instability challenges in multi-robot anti-winding control under complex constrained environments.

Abstract: In the field of precision manufacturing in complex constrained environments, the role of soft robots is increasingly prominent, and the realization of anti-winding control based on multi-intelligent body reinforcement learning has become a research hotspot. One of the core problems at present is to coordinate multiple robots to complete the unwinding operation in a highly constrained environment. The existing distributed training framework faces some observability challenges in high-density barrier and unstable environments, resulting in poor learning results. This paper proposes a topology-driven Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (TD-MARL) framework to coordinate multi-robot systems to avoid entanglement. Specifically, the critical network adopts centralized learning, so that each intelligent body can perceive the strategies of other intelligent bodies by sharing the topological state, thus alleviating the training instability caused by complex interactions; eliminating the demand for communication resources between robots through distributed execution, Upgrade system reliability; the integrated topological security layer uses topological invariants to accurately assess and mitigate the risk of entanglement to avoid the strategy from falling into local difficulties. Finally, the full simulation experiments carried out in the real simulation environment show that the method is better than the current advanced deep reinforcement learning (DRL) method in terms of convergence and anti-winding effect.

</details>


### [3] [DexSim2Real: Foundation Model-Guided Sim-to-Real Transfer for Generalizable Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05241)
*Zijian Zeng,Fei Ding,Huiming Yang,Xianwei Li,Yuhao Liao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DexSim2Real bridges the sim-to-real gap for dexterous manipulation using vision-language foundation models for domain randomization, tactile-visual cross-attention policy, and progressive skill curriculum, achieving 78.2% real-world success rate with only 8.3% sim-to-real performance gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sim-to-real transfer is a critical bottleneck for deploying dexterous manipulation policies from simulation to real robots; existing approaches using manual domain randomization or task-specific adaptation lack generalizability across diverse scenarios.

Method: (1) FM-DR: vision-language model as visual realism critic optimizing simulation parameters via closed-loop CMA-ES; (2) TVCAP: cross-attention visuo-tactile fusion for zero-shot sim-to-real RL; (3) PSC: LLM-based task decomposition with difficulty scheduler for contact-rich tasks.

Result: 78.2% average real-world success rate across six manipulation tasks, outperforming DrEureka and DeXtreme with only 8.3% sim-to-real performance gap.

Conclusion: DexSim2Real effectively bridges sim-to-real gap for dexterous manipulation by integrating foundation models for domain randomization, tactile-visual policy, and curriculum learning, achieving strong generalization with minimal performance drop.

Abstract: Sim-to-real transfer remains a critical bottleneck for deploying dexterous manipulation policies learned in simulation to real-world robots. Existing approaches rely on manually designed domain randomization or task-specific adaptation, limiting their generalizability across diverse manipulation scenarios. We present DexSim2Real, an integrated framework that leverages vision-language foundation models to bridge the sim-to-real gap for dexterous manipulation. Our system combines three components: (1) Foundation Model-Guided Domain Randomization (FM-DR), which uses a vision-language model as a visual realism critic to optimize simulation parameters via closed-loop CMA-ES, complementing text-based approaches like DrEureka with direct visual feedback; (2) a Tactile-Visual Cross-Attention Policy (TVCAP) that adapts cross-attention visuo-tactile fusion to zero-shot sim-to-real RL; and (3) a Progressive Skill Curriculum (PSC) that builds on LLM-based task decomposition with a difficulty scheduler tailored to contact-rich dexterous tasks. Extensive experiments on six challenging manipulation tasks with blinded evaluation demonstrate that DexSim2Real achieves a 78.2% average real-world success rate, outperforming DrEureka and DeXtreme while reducing the sim-to-real performance gap to only 8.3%.

</details>


### [4] [Track A*: Fast Visibility-Aware Trajectory Planning for Active Target Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05338)
*Hanxuan Chen,Kangli Wang,Ji Pei*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TA star is an offline search-based trajectory planner for active target tracking that uses a layered DAG search with beam pruning, BVH-based distance caching, and multi-ray visibility evaluation. It achieves 100% convergence (vs 56.9% for A* baseline) with 23.0x mean speedup and negligible visibility loss (-0.15 pp), though it sacrifices theoretical optimality for scalability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Offline reference trajectories are needed for building multi-modal tracking datasets and benchmarking online tracking planners under repeatable conditions, but existing methods lack efficient visibility-aware planning on spatio-temporal grids.

Method: TA star operates on a discretized 4D spatio-temporal grid (x, y, z, t) using a layered Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG) search with three optimizations: (1) cross-time obstacle distance caching against a Bounding Volume Hierarchy (BVH), (2) per-layer beam pruning, and (3) a configurable multi-ray visibility evaluator.

Result: On 1000 scenarios across 8 CARLA maps: 100% convergence, 45s completion with 32 workers. On 248-scenario comparison: 23.0x mean / 11.8x worst-case speedup, convergence improved from 56.9% to 100%. On 141 baseline-converged subset: average visibility change of -0.15 pp, max drop <5 pp.

Conclusion: TA star is a practical offline reference planner for active target tracking that trades strict optimality for scalability, with limitations in challenging environments like dense vegetation (Town07).

Abstract: Offline reference trajectories for active target tracking are needed both for building multi-modal tracking datasets and for benchmarking online tracking planners under repeatable conditions. We present Track A star (TA star), an offline search-based trajectory planner that targets the visibility-aware target tracking objective on a discretized four-dimensional spatio-temporal grid (x, y, z, t). TA star combines a layered Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG) search with three engineering optimizations: cross-time obstacle distance caching against a Bounding Volume Hierarchy (BVH), per-layer beam pruning, and a configurable multi-ray visibility evaluator. TA star employs a beam-pruned heuristic search on this discrete graph to efficiently find high-quality tracking trajectories. While it trades strict theoretical optimality for practical scalability, our empirical results demonstrate robust, near-baseline visibility performance at a fraction of the computational cost. On a 1000-scenario stress test across eight CARLA Optimized maps, TA star converges on all scenarios and completes in 45 s using 32 workers; on a 248-scenario controlled comparison against an unoptimized priority-queue A star baseline (BinaryHeap implementation) under identical scenario inputs and a 5 x 10^6 expansion cap, TA star reduces mean planning time by 23.0x and worst-case planning time by 11.8x, while raising convergence from 56.9% to 100%. On the n=141 baseline-converged subset, TA star changes average visibility by only -0.15 percentage points (pp), with no scenario exceeding a 5 pp drop. We position TA star as a practical offline reference planner under these specific conditions, with limitations and failure cases discussed for environments such as Town07 dense vegetation.

</details>


### [5] [Passive Fault Tolerance through Tension-to-Thrust Feed-Forward: Hybrid Input-to-State Stability for Decentralized Multi-UAV Slung-Load Transport under Abrupt Cable Severance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05339)
*Hadi Hajieghrary,Paul Schmitt*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a passive decentralized control architecture for multi-UAV slung-load transport that uses direct cable tension feed-forward into altitude thrust commands, combined with a PD anti-swing projection cascade, to enable certified recovery from abrupt cable severance without requiring inter-vehicle communication or reconfiguration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-UAV slung-load controllers rely on coordinated force allocation, peer-state exchange, or fixed cable topology, lacking a certified decentralized recovery mechanism for unannounced cable severance, which leaves limited time for fault diagnosis and reconfiguration.

Method: A passive architecture routes each vehicle's measured cable tension directly into its altitude thrust command (T_i^ff = T_i), uses a PD, anti-swing, and projection cascade for local tracking, and provides a conditional hybrid practical input-to-state-stability (pISS) certificate with slack-excursion-bounded taut-cable reduction, bounded Lyapunov jumps, inter-fault decay, and per-fault-cycle contraction ρ ∈ (0,1).

Result: Validated in Drake multibody simulation with 5 vehicles, 10 kg payload, Kelvin-Voigt cables, Dryden wind: 0.312-0.328 m RMSE, 76.1-95.2 mm peak sag, recovery within one payload-pendulum period. Disabling identity feed-forward inflates cruise error by 34-39% and peak sag by 3.6x-4.0x.

Conclusion: The local tension feed-forward is identified as the dominant passive recovery mechanism in the tested decentralized cascade, enabling certified recovery from both single and dual cable severance events.

Abstract: Abrupt cable severance in multi-UAV slung-load transport redistributes load and changes the active constraint set, leaving limited time for fault diagnosis and reconfiguration. Existing controllers rely on coordinated force allocation, peer-state exchange, or fixed cable topology, and therefore lack a certified decentralized recovery mechanism for unannounced severance. We present a passive architecture that routes each vehicle's measured cable tension directly into its altitude thrust command, $T_i^{\mathrm{ff}}=T_i$, while a surrounding proportional-derivative, anti-swing, and projection cascade preserves local tracking feasibility. The main contribution is a conditional hybrid practical input-to-state-stability certificate that composes a slack-excursion-bounded taut-cable reduction, bounded post-severance Lyapunov jumps, inter-fault decay, and per-fault-cycle contraction $ρ\in (0,1)$ into an explicit recovery envelope under stated actuator, slack, and dwell assumptions. We validate the controller in Drake multibody simulation with five vehicles, a 10 kg payload, Kelvin-Voigt cables, Dryden wind, and single- and dual-severance schedules: the closed loop attains 0.312-0.328 m RMSE, 76.1-95.2 mm peak sag, and recovery within one payload-pendulum period. Disabling the identity inflates cruise error by 34-39% and peak sag by 3.6x-4.0x, identifying local tension feed-forward as the dominant passive recovery mechanism in the tested decentralized cascade.

</details>


### [6] [Creative Robot Tool Use by Counterfactual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05411)
*M. Tuluhan Akbulut,Varun Satheesh,Ahmed Jaafar,Alper Ahmetoglu,Shane Parr,Aditya Ganeshan,Shivam Vats,George Konidaris*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A causal reasoning framework for creative robot tool use that discovers causal relationships between tools and tasks through simulated experiments in a dynamics model, combining VLM-based feature suggestions with counterfactual tool generation via geometric and physical perturbations, enabling reliable tool selection and skill transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable robots to identify and use novel tools for tasks beyond their primary objectives by understanding causal relationships grounded in physics, rather than relying on superficial visual features.

Method: The framework decouples causal discovery into two components: (1) VLM-based feature suggestion and (2) counterfactual tool generation via targeted geometric/physical feature perturbations. Causal relationships are discovered through simulated experiments in a dynamics model. Novel objects are classified based on identified causal features, and tool use skills are transferred via keypoint matching conditioned on these features.

Result: The approach successfully demonstrated tool use in reaching distant objects with sticks, scooping candies from a bowl, and using boxes/crates as stepping platforms. Baseline comparisons showed that identifying causal features grounded in physical tool properties leads to more reliable tool selection and stronger skill keypoint transfer.

Conclusion: Grounded causal reasoning in physical tool properties improves robot tool use reliability and generality compared to methods that lack explicit causal feature identification.

Abstract: We propose a causal reasoning framework for creative robot tool use where a suitable tool for a task is correctly identified for use beyond its primary objectives. The proposed framework first discovers the causal relationships between the tool and the task by conducting simulated experiments in a dynamics model. We decouple the causal discovery problem into two complementary components: VLM-based feature suggestion and counterfactual tool generation via targeted geometric and physical feature perturbations. Then, novel objects are classified based on identified causal features, and the tool use skill is transferred via keypoint matching conditioned on the identified causal features. By reconstructing the task in a dynamics model, our approach grounds tool use in the physics of the problem. We illustrate our approach in reaching a distant object with different sticks, scooping candies from a bowl using diverse items, and using different boxes or crates as stepping platforms to retrieve an object from a high shelf. Our baseline comparisons show that identifying causal features and grounding them in physical tool properties leads to more reliable tool selection and stronger skill keypoint transfer.

</details>


### [7] [Contact-Free Grasp Stability Prediction with In-Hand Time-of-Flight Sensors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05461)
*Kyle DuFrene,Cindy Grimm*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A contact-free grasp stability predictor using multi-zone time-of-flight sensors in gripper distal links, achieving ~86% accuracy at 15 Hz without requiring object grasping.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing tactile-based grasp stability classifiers require making contact and grasping the object to detect failures, which is slow and prone to degradation with noisy sensors.

Method: Multi-zone time-of-flight sensors mounted in gripper distal links; collected over 2,500 real-world grasps across 15 objects to train a classifier; validated on 3 unseen objects and tested on 3 additional unseen objects.

Result: 85.5% accuracy on validation objects, 86.0% accuracy on test objects; classification cycling at 15 Hz.

Conclusion: Contact-free grasp stability prediction is feasible and significantly faster than contact-based methods, enabling rapid stability assessment before grasping occurs.

Abstract: Current approaches to grasp planning for robotics demonstrate high success rates, but degrade with noisy sensors and other factors. Previous works have proposed tactile-based grasp stability classifiers to detect failures, but these approaches rely on making contact and grasping the object to do so. We propose a contact-free grasp stability predictor using multi-zone time-of-flight sensors mounted in the distal links of a gripper. Our method, as it does not require grasping the object to make a prediction, significantly speeds up the stability classification process, cycling at 15 Hz. We collected over 2,500 real-world grasps across 15 objects to train a classifier. Additionally, we conducted grasp attempts over six additional unseen objects, three for validation and model selection, and three for model testing. Our approach demonstrated strong classification performance, with an accuracy of 85.5% on validation and 86.0% on test objects.

</details>


### [8] [Robust $\mathcal{H}_\infty$ Controller Design For INDI-Controlled Quadrotor Using Online Parameter Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05483)
*Tom Aantjes,Till M. Blaha,Spilios Theodoulis,Ewoud J. J. Smeur*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a robust gain-scheduled controller for quadrotor attitude control using INDI-based inner loop with online parameter identification, synthesized via ℋ∞ closed-loop shaping, and validated through simulations and flight tests showing acceptable performance even with parameters outside the defined uncertainty range for actuator time constants below 40 ms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While INDI controller parameters can be estimated onboard a multirotor quickly enough for in-flight identification, a robust method to tune outer loop gains for this feedback-linearizing INDI controller depending on model parameters is still missing.

Method: Design of a robust gain-scheduled cascaded attitude controller with feedforward filter for a symmetric quadrotor using signal-based ℋ∞ closed-loop shaping, with an INDI-based inner loop and online identification of system parameters.

Result: Good stability margins and effective tracking performance under uncertainty in nonlinear simulations. Flight tests with full online parameter identification showed acceptable performance for actuator time constants below 40 ms, even when identified parameters were far outside the defined uncertainty range.

Conclusion: The proposed gain-scheduled controller is robust and maintains acceptable flight performance despite significant parameter uncertainty, provided actuator time constants remain below 40 ms.

Abstract: It has recently been shown that all physical parameters of an Incremental Nonlinear Dynamic Inversion (INDI) controller can be estimated onboard a multirotor within half a second, which is fast enough to do the full identification during a throw in the air. However, a robust method to tune outer loop gains for this feedback-linearizing INDI controller depending on the model parameters is still missing. This work presents the design of a robust gain-scheduled controller for attitude control of quadrotor, using an INDI-based inner loop with online identification of its system parameters. A gain-scheduled cascaded attitude controller with a feedforward filter is synthesized for a symmetric quadrotor using signal-based $\mathcal{H}_\infty$ closed-loop shaping. The resulting controller exhibits good stability margins, with nonlinear simulations confirming effective tracking performance under uncertainty. Experimental evaluation is also conducted through flight tests with full online parameter identification. Even though the identified parameters during these tests are far outside the defined uncertainty range, acceptable flight performance comparable to simulation results is maintained for actuator time constants below 40 ms.

</details>


### [9] [Real-world Latency Analysis of Vehicular Visible Light Communication with Multiple LED Transmitters and an Event-Based Camera](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05541)
*Ryota Soga,Tsukasa Shimizu,Shintaro Shiba,Quan Kong,Shan Lu,Takaya Yamazato*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Event-camera-based VLC system for V2X applications addresses bandwidth saturation, multi-transmitter reception, and latency characterization, demonstrating feasible complement to existing V2X technologies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Event cameras offer high temporal resolution, low latency, and wide dynamic range, making them promising receivers for VLC in V2X applications, but key challenges (bandwidth saturation, multi-transmitter reception, latency) need to be addressed.

Method: Positive-event-only mode with a protocol suppressing event generation while maintaining communication distance and wide FOV; method to identify multiple transmitters; end-to-end latency evaluation in real vehicular scenarios.

Result: Simultaneous reception from up to three LEDs; system meets cooperative perception latency requirements.

Conclusion: Event-camera-based VLC is a feasible complement to existing V2X technologies (e.g., RF).

Abstract: Event cameras offer high temporal resolution, low latency, and wide dynamic range, making them promising receivers for visible light communication (VLC) in vehicle-to-everything (V2X) applications. This work presents an event-camera-based VLC system addressing three key challenges: bandwidth saturation, multi-transmitter reception, and latency characterization.
  We adopt a positive-event-only mode and design a protocol that suppresses event generation while maintaining communication distance and a wide field of view. We also propose a method to identify multiple transmitters and demonstrate simultaneous reception from up to three LEDs. Finally, we evaluate end-to-end latency in real vehicular scenarios and show that the system meets cooperative perception requirements. These results demonstrate that event-camera-based VLC is a feasible complement to existing V2X technologies (e.g., RF).

</details>


### [10] [On the Emergence of Pendular Structure in Multi-Contact Locomotion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05707)
*Lingxue Lyu,Zihui Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper shows that LIPM emerges naturally as the optimal solution of a centroidal OCP that penalizes angular momentum rate, rather than being an arbitrary modeling choice. It derives closed-form predictions for pendular force patterns based on foot-span geometry, identifies feasibility limits in trot gaits, and validates these claims on a point-mass quadruped and Unitree Go1 in simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge the gap between the widespread use of LIPM in legged locomotion control and the lack of explicit justification that controllers actually prefer LIPM-like behavior, by showing LIPM emerges naturally from optimizing a simple centroidal OCP with angular momentum rate penalties.

Method: Formulates a small centroidal OCP penalizing rate of angular momentum, analyzes its optimum via SVD of the moment Jacobian for full-rank stance and N=2 stance (trot), derives closed-form bounds and feasibility kinks, and validates on point-mass quadruped and Unitree Go1 in MuJoCo using both open-loop QP and torque-level closed-loop control.

Result: (1) For full-rank stance, optimum drifts toward pendular force pattern with rate set by SVD of moment Jacobian; constant matches experiments within 16%. (2) For N=2 stance (trot), friction cone imposes a lower bound on angular momentum rate that weight tuning cannot fix, with a closed-form critical horizontal acceleration causing a non-smooth feasibility kink. (3) Adding a task term for nonzero angular momentum rate predictably shifts the optimum off the pendular set.

Conclusion: The LIPM behavior is not an arbitrary modeling choice but emerges as the optimal solution under angular momentum rate penalization. The results align closely with classical ZMP/DCM picture, though the asymptotic analysis has limitations in describing closed-loop behavior in practice.

Abstract: LIPM is everywhere in legged-locomotion control, but almost always as a modeling choice rather than as something the
  controller's cost actually prefers. This note tries to make that link more explicit. Working from a small centroidal
  OCP that penalizes the rate of angular momentum, we look at what its optimum tends to look like. Three things come
  out. With full-rank stance, the optimum drifts toward a pendular force pattern at a rate determined by the SVD of the
  moment Jacobian; the constant is set by foot-span geometry and matches the experiments to within 16%. With N=2 stance,
  as in trot, the friction cone introduces a lower bound on $\|\dot{H}_G\|$ that no amount of weight tuning fixes; we
  also see a non-smooth feasibility kink at a critical horizontal acceleration that we can write in closed form. Adding
  a task term that asks for a nonzero $\dot{H}_G$ moves the optimum off the pendular set in a predictable way. None of
  this is far from the classical ZMP/DCM picture. We test these claims on a point-mass quadruped and on the Unitree Go1
  in MuJoCo (open-loop QP and a torque-level closed-loop controller), and we note where the asymptotic story stops being
  a good description of what the closed loop actually does.

</details>


### [11] [MaMi-HOI: Harmonizing Global Kinematics and Local Geometry for Human-Object Interaction Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05756)
*Hao Wang,Shiqi Wang,Qi Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MaMi-HOI is a hierarchical framework for generating realistic 3D Human-Object Interactions that addresses "Geometric Forgetting" in diffusion models—where semantic features overshadow object geometry. It uses a Geometry-Aware Proximity Adapter (GAPA) for precise contact correction and a Kinematic Harmony Adapter (KHA) to maintain natural whole-body motion, achieving both natural movement and precise contact for long-term complex tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for 3D Human-Object Interaction generation excel at semantic alignment but struggle to maintain precise object contact due to a phenomenon called "Geometric Forgetting," where increasing diffusion model depth causes semantic features to overshadow object geometry features, losing perception of object geometry.

Method: MaMi-HOI is a hierarchical framework with two key components: (1) Geometry-Aware Proximity Adapter (GAPA), which re-injects dense object geometry details to perform residual snapping corrections for precise contact; (2) Kinematic Harmony Adapter (KHA), which proactively aligns whole-body posture with spatial objectives to ensure natural skeleton motion and prevent robotic stiffness caused by aggressive local enforcement.

Result: Extensive experiments validate that MaMi-HOI simultaneously achieves natural motion and precise contact, and extends generation capabilities to long-term tasks with complex trajectories, bridging global navigation and high-fidelity manipulation in 3D scenes.

Conclusion: MaMi-HOI effectively reconciles macro-level kinematic fluidity with micro-level spatial precision, overcoming the geometric forgetting problem in diffusion models for 3D HOI generation, enabling both natural motion and precise object contact for complex, long-term interaction tasks.

Abstract: Generating realistic 3D Human-Object Interactions (HOI) is a fundamental task for applications ranging from embodied AI to virtual content creation, which requires harmonizing high-level semantic intent with strict low-level physical constraints. Existing methods excel at semantic alignment, however, they struggle to maintain precise object contact. We reveal a key finding termed \textit{Geometric Forgetting}: as diffusion model depth increases, semantic feature tend to overshadow object geometry feature, causing the model to lose its perception to object geometry. To address this, we propose MaMi-HOI, a hierarchical framework reconciling \textbf{Ma}cro-level kinematic fluidity with \textbf{Mi}cro-level spatial precision. First, to counteract geometric forgetting, we introduce the Geometry-Aware Proximity Adapter (GAPA), which explicitly re-injects dense object details to perform residual snapping corrections for precise contact. Nevertheless, such aggressive local enforcement can disrupt global dynamics, leading to robotic stiffness. In response, we introduce the Kinematic Harmony Adapter (KHA), which proactively aligns whole-body posture with spatial objectives, ensuring the skeleton actively accommodates constraints without compromising naturalness. Extensive experiments validate that MaMi-HOI simultaneously achieves natural motion and precise contact. Crucially, it extends generation capabilities to long-term tasks with complex trajectories, effectively bridging the gap between global navigation and high-fidelity manipulation in 3D scenes. Code is available at https://github.com/DON738110198/MaMi-HOI.git

</details>


### [12] [Resource-Constrained Robotic Planning in the face of Mixed Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05797)
*Yihao Yin,Pian Yu,Andrea Turrini,Zhiming Chi,Yong Li,Lijun Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper presents a framework (CMDPST) and methods for synthesizing robust robot strategies that maximize the probability of completing tasks specified in LTLf while never exhausting resources, validated on a warehouse transportation network.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robots must operate under both quantifiable (noise) and unquantifiable uncertainty while respecting strict resource constraints; existing methods lack a unified approach to handle nondeterministic actions, mixed uncertainty types, and resource exhaustion simultaneously.

Method: Models the robotic system as a Consumption Markov Decision Process with Set-valued Transitions (CMDPST), combines it with LTLf task specifications, and solves the resource-constrained optimal robust strategy synthesis problem using a direct unrolling-based method and an optimized approach with state-space pruning.

Result: Experiments on a warehouse transportation network demonstrate the effectiveness of both the direct and optimized solutions.

Conclusion: The proposed CMDPST framework and synthesis techniques enable robots to maximize task satisfaction probability under mixed uncertainty types while strictly avoiding resource exhaustion.

Abstract: Robots operate under significant uncertainty, from quantifiable noise to unquantifiable unknowns, and must account for strict operational constraints, such as limited resources. In this paper, we consider the problem of synthesizing robust strategies to guide a robot's actions in fulfilling a given task, while ensuring the system never exhausts its resources. To solve this problem, we first model the robotic system as a Consumption Markov Decision Process with Set-valued Transitions(CMDPST), a unified framework modelling nondeterministic actions, quantifiable and unquantifiable uncertainty, and resource consumption. Then, we combine the CMDPST with the task specification, expressed as a Linear Temporal Logic over finite traces (LTLf ) formula. Lastly, we address the resource constrained optimal robust strategy synthesis problem, which aims to synthesize a strategy that maximizes the probability of satisfying the LTLf objective without resource exhaustion. Our solution involves two techniques: a direct unrolling-based method and a more efficient, optimized approach that leverages state-space pruning for better performance. Experiments on a warehouse transportation network show the effectiveness of the proposed solutions.

</details>


### [13] [A Comparative Study of INDI and NDI with Nonlinear Disturbance Observer for Aerial Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05825)
*Benedetta Rota,Mirko Mizzoni,Amr Afifi,Pieter van Goor,Antonio Franchi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper compares INDI and NDI+NDO control strategies for fully actuated aerial robots through simulation, finding INDI more robust under model-mismatch and combined-stress conditions while NDI+NDO shows greater sensitivity in non-ideal scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a systematic comparative robustness analysis of two popular nonlinear control approaches (INDI vs NDI with disturbance observer) for fully actuated aerial robots, offering practical guidance on their relative strengths and limitations.

Method: Conducted a simulation-based comparative analysis using a systematic simulation campaign across representative operating scenarios. Compared tracking performance, robustness, and control effort under parametric variations, external disturbances, and measurement noise.

Result: INDI demonstrates stronger robustness in several model-mismatch and combined-stress cases, while NDI+NDO primarily matches nominal performance but exhibits greater sensitivity under several non-ideal conditions.

Conclusion: The findings provide practical guidance on the relative strengths and limitations of incremental (INDI) versus observer-based (NDI+NDO) inversion strategies for aerial robotic applications.

Abstract: This work presents a simulation-based comparative robustness analysis of Incremental Nonlinear Dynamic Inversion (INDI) and Nonlinear Dynamic Inversion augmented with a nonlinear disturbance observer (NDI+NDO) for fully actuated aerial robots. A systematic simulation campaign across representative operating scenarios is conducted, where we compare tracking performance, robustness, control effort, under parametric variations, external disturbances, and measurement noise. Results show that INDI demonstrates stronger robustness in several model-mismatch and combined-stress cases, while NDI+NDO primarily matches nominal performance but exhibits greater sensitivity under several non-ideal conditions. These findings provide practical guidance on the relative strengths and limitations of incremental and observer-based inversion strategies for aerial robotic applications.

</details>


### [14] [Cycle-resolved Cephalopod-Inspired Pulsed-Jet Robot With High-Volume Expulsion and Drag-Reduced Gliding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05875)
*Yiyuan Zhang,Anye Zhong,Junkai Chen,Wenci Xin,Cecilia Laschi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a cephalopod-inspired pulsed-jet robot with a rigid-soft hybrid origami mantle that achieves large, actively driven body deformation. The robot reaches ~0.5 m/s peak speed (3.8 BL/s) and >0.2 m/s average speed, with a cycle-resolved framework analyzing how expelled volume, glide duration, and refill pathway affect locomotion performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cephalopod pulsed-jet locomotion involves a coordinated cycle of jet expulsion, passive gliding, and mantle refilling, yet most prior robotic systems treat jetting as isolated expulsion events rather than cycle-resolved dynamics. This work aims to bridge that gap with a bio-inspired platform.

Method: A rigid-soft hybrid origami mantle combining rigid folding panels with a compliant silicone framework, enabling 75% cavity-volume reduction during expulsion and ~75.7% reduction in projected cross-sectional drag area during gliding. A cycle-resolved experimental framework separately investigates expelled volume, glide duration, and refill pathway.

Result: Peak speed ~0.5 m/s (3.8 BL/s), average speed >0.2 m/s (1.5 BL/s) within the first jetting cycle. Demonstrated roles of high expelled-volume-ratio contraction in speed generation, reduced-drag gliding, and mantle-aperture-inspired passive inlet valves for refill assistance.

Conclusion: Provides a robotic implementation of actively deformable cephalopod-like jet propulsion and a unified experimental platform for studying expulsion-gliding-refilling dynamics in pulsed-jet locomotion.

Abstract: Cephalopod pulsed-jet locomotion is not a single isolated expulsion event, but a coordinated cycle involving jet expulsion, passive gliding, and mantle refilling. Inspired by this cycle-resolved biological strategy, this paper presents a cephalopod-inspired pulsed-jet robot with a rigid-soft hybrid origami mantle that enables large, actively driven, and geometry-guided body deformation. The proposed mantle integrates rigid folding panels with a compliant silicone framework, allowing a 75% effective cavity-volume reduction during expulsion and reducing the projected cross-sectional drag area by approximately 75.7% in the contracted gliding configuration. Using this platform, we formulate a cycle-resolved framework to separately investigate how expelled volume, glide duration, and refill pathway influence whole-cycle locomotion performance. Experiments show that the robot reaches a peak speed of approximately 0.5 m/s (3.8 BL/s) and an average speed exceeding 0.2 m/s (1.5 BL/s) within the first jetting cycle. The results further demonstrate the roles of high expelled-volume-ratio contraction in speed generation, reduced-drag-area gliding under different glide durations, and mantle-aperture-inspired passive inlet valves in assisting refill. This work provides both a robotic implementation of actively deformable cephalopod-like jet propulsion and a unified experimental platform for studying expulsion-gliding-refilling dynamics in pulsed-jet locomotion.

</details>


### [15] [Generating Roadside LiDAR Datasets from Vehicle-Side Datasets via Novel View Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05897)
*Yuhan Xia,Runxin Zhao,Hanyang Zhuang,Chunxiang Wang,Ming Yang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VRS is a data synthesis framework that generates labeled roadside LiDAR datasets from vehicle-side data using novel view synthesis, addressing the scarcity of annotated roadside data for perception models in Intelligent Transportation Systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The scarcity of large-scale annotated roadside LiDAR datasets hinders the training of high-performance roadside perception models for ITS.

Method: VRS employs vehicle point cloud completion to compensate for missing geometry and introduces an occupancy-based visibility constraint to handle large viewpoint changes during cross-view rendering, enabling flexible multi-view synthesis from vehicle-side to roadside perspectives.

Result: Synthesized data effectively complements real roadside data, improves roadside 3D object detection performance, mitigates limited real-world data issues, and enhances generalization to unseen roadside viewpoints.

Conclusion: VRS provides a scalable solution for generating labeled roadside LiDAR data, reducing reliance on costly manual annotation and improving roadside perception model robustness.

Abstract: Intelligent Transportation Systems (ITS) require reliable environmental perception to support safe and efficient transportation. With the rapid development of Vehicle-to-everything (V2X), roadside perception has become an effective means to extend sensing coverage and improve traffic safety. However, the scarcity of large-scale annotated roadside LiDAR datasets poses a major challenge for training high-performance roadside perception models. In this paper, we introduce Vehicle-to-Roadside LiDAR Synthesis (VRS), a data synthesis framework that generates labeled roadside LiDAR datasets from vehicle-side datasets via LiDAR novel view synthesis. To mitigate the vehicle-to-roadside domain gap, VRS employs vehicle point cloud completion to compensate for missing geometry in vehicle-side observations, and introduces an occupancy-based visibility constraint to handle large viewpoint changes during cross-view rendering. The proposed framework enables flexible multi-view rendering for scalable roadside data generation. Extensive experiments on roadside 3D object detection demonstrate that the synthesized data effectively complements real roadside data, mitigates the limitations of limited real-world roadside data, and improves generalization to unseen roadside viewpoints.

</details>


### [16] [DexSynRefine: Synthesizing and Refining Human-Object Interaction Motion for Physically Feasible Dexterous Robot Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05925)
*Hyesung Lee,Hyunwoo Jung,Si-Hwan Heo,Sungwook Yang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DexSynRefine combines motion manifold primitives with task-space residual RL and contact-dynamics adaptation to synthesize and physically ground dexterous manipulation trajectories from sparse human-object interaction data, achieving 50-70% improvement over kinematic retargeting across five tasks in sim-to-real transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning dexterous manipulation from human-object interaction (HOI) data is scalable but faces challenges: HOI demonstrations are sparse, provide only kinematic motion not directly executable under embodiment mismatch (robot hand vs. human hand) and contact-rich dynamics.

Method: Three coupled components: (1) HOI-MMFP: task- and object-initial-state-conditioned motion manifold primitives for synthesizing coordinated hand-object trajectories from sparse HOI demos; (2) Task-space residual RL policy that grounds the synthesized reference physically while preserving kinematic structure; (3) Contact-and-dynamics adaptation module enabling sim-to-real transfer from proprioceptive history.

Result: Outperforms prior action-representation baselines in simulation and transfers to real robot across all five dexterous manipulation tasks (pick-and-place, tool use, object reorientation), improving over kinematic retargeting by 50-70 percentage points.

Conclusion: The framework effectively bridges the gap between sparse kinematic HOI demonstrations and executable dexterous manipulation policies, with strong sim-to-real transfer capabilities.

Abstract: Learning dexterous manipulation from human-object interaction (HOI) data is a scalable alternative to teleoperation, but HOI demonstrations are sparse and provide only kinematic motion that is not directly executable under embodiment mismatch and contact-rich dynamics. We present DexSynRefine, a framework with three coupled components: HOI-MMFP, a task- and object-initial-state-conditioned extension of motion manifold primitives that synthesizes coordinated hand-object trajectories from sparse HOI demonstrations; a task-space residual RL policy that physically grounds the synthesized reference while inheriting its kinematic structure; and a contact-and-dynamics adaptation module that enables sim-to-real transfer from proprioceptive history. Across five dexterous manipulation tasks spanning pick-and-place, tool use, and object reorientation, our task-space residual policy outperforms prior action-representation baselines in simulations and transfers to a real robot on all five tasks, improving over kinematic retargeting by 50-70 percentage points.

</details>


### [17] [Plug-and-Play Label Map Diffusion for Universal Goal-Oriented Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05960)
*Zhixuan Shen,Yijie Zeng,Shengxiang Luo,Tianrui Li,Haonan Luo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PLMD is a plug-and-play diffusion-based map completion model that generates obstacle and semantic labels for unobserved regions, enabling better goal localization in partially explored environments for Goal-Oriented Navigation. It achieves SOTA on three GON tasks and integrates seamlessly with existing semantic map-based navigation strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing map-based GON methods rely on self-centered semantic maps that suffer from incomplete maps and inconsistent semantic association, limiting goal localization in partially observed environments.

Method: PLMD uses a Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Model (DDPM) to perform map completion diffusion, generating both obstacle and semantic labels for unobserved regions. It leverages structural consistency between known and unknown obstacle layouts and integrates obstacle priors into the semantic denoising process to mitigate inconsistent semantic associations.

Result: PLMD (I) effectively expands the region of unknown maps, (II) integrates seamlessly into existing navigation strategies relying on semantic maps, (III) achieves state-of-the-art performance on three GON tasks.

Conclusion: The proposed plug-and-play label map diffusion model enables accurate goal localization in partially observed environments by substituting predicted labels for unobserved regions, outperforming existing methods across multiple GON benchmarks.

Abstract: In embodied vision, Goal-Oriented Navigation (GON) requires robots to locate a specific goal within an unexplored environment. The primary challenge of GON arises from the need to construct a Bird's-Eye-View (BEV) map to understand the environment while simultaneously localizing an unobserved goal. Existing map-based methods typically employ self-centered semantic maps, often facing challenges such as reliance on complete maps or inconsistent semantic association. To this end, we propose Plug-and-Play Label Map Diffusion (PLMD), which defines a novel map completion diffusion model based on Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models (DDPM). PLMD generates obstacle and semantic labels for unobserved regions through a diffusion-based completion process, thereby enabling goal localization even in partially observed environments. Moreover, it mitigates inconsistent semantic association by leveraging structural consistency between known and unknown obstacle layouts and integrating obstacle priors into the semantic denoising process. By substituting predicted labels for unobserved regions, robots can accurately localize the specified objects. Extensive experiments demonstrate that PLMD \textbf{(I)} effectively expands the region of unknown maps, \textbf{(II)} integrates seamlessly into existing navigation strategies that rely on semantic maps, \textbf{(III)} achieves state-of-the-art performance on three GON tasks.

</details>


### [18] [Accurate Trajectory Tracking with MPCC for Flapping-Wing MAVs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06042)
*Charbel Toumieh,Jack Zeng,Niel Mistry,Dario Floreano*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A Model Predictive Contouring Control (MPCC) approach for flapping-wing micro aerial vehicles that tracks arc-length-parameterized trajectories while optimizing progress online, using a compact differentiable dynamical model capturing dominant couplings, achieving ~6.5-9 cm tracking error (10x improvement over prior methods) at up to 3 m/s on the XFly ornithopter.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bird-scale ornithopters suffer from tightly coupled lift, airspeed, and turning authority governed by few control inputs; conventional cascaded controllers produce persistent tracking errors during complex maneuvers, and existing methods cannot robustly produce predefined speed profiles needed for time-parameterized trajectory tracking.

Method: Model Predictive Contouring Control (MPCC) tracking arc-length-parameterized trajectories with online progress optimization, paired with a compact, continuously differentiable dynamical model that captures dominant aerodynamic couplings of bird-scale ornithopters while remaining computationally tractable for real-time nonlinear optimization.

Result: Validated on XFly ornithopter along circular and 3D racing trajectories; achieved mean deviation of 6.5-9 cm at speeds up to 3 m/s, representing nearly a 10-fold improvement over prior ornithopter control methods.

Conclusion: The proposed MPCC framework effectively addresses coupled ornithopter dynamics without requiring predefined speed profiles, significantly outperforming conventional cascaded controllers and enabling precise autonomous control of bird-scale flapping-wing vehicles.

Abstract: Flapping-wing micro aerial vehicles offer quieter and safer operation than rotary-wing drones, yet achieving precise autonomous control of bird-scale ornithopters remains challenging: lift, airspeed, and turning authority are tightly coupled and governed by only a few control inputs. Conventional cascaded controllers treat altitude, speed, and heading independently, producing persistent tracking errors during complex maneuvers, while time-parameterized trajectory tracking requires predefined speed profiles that existing methods cannot robustly produce for these coupled dynamics. We address both limitations simultaneously with a Model Predictive Contouring Control (MPCC) approach that tracks arc-length-parameterized trajectories while optimizing progress online, eliminating the need for predefined timing. However, MPCC requires a dynamical model that captures the coupled aerodynamics without exceeding the computational budget of real-time nonlinear optimization. Here, we propose a compact, continuously differentiable model that captures the dominant couplings of bird-scale ornithopters, enabling real-time predictive control. We validated the method with the XFly ornithopter flying along circular and three-dimensional racing trajectories and achieved a mean deviation from the reference trajectory between 6.5 and 9 cm at speeds up to 3 m/s, which represents an almost 10-fold improvement over prior ornithopter control methods.

</details>


### [19] [Monitoring autonomous persistent surveillance missions using invariance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06062)
*Vladislav Nenchev,Prodromos Sotiriadis*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a compositional runtime monitor for persistent surveillance by autonomous robots with black-box autonomy stacks, using decentralized low-dimensional invariant sets computed offline and checked online, demonstrated on a real robot monitoring a labyrinth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Persistent surveillance by autonomous robots with black-box autonomy stacks lacks formal guarantees; computing a full-system invariant for large environments is computationally intractable.

Method: The environment is partitioned into finite uncertainty regions. The closed-loop system is modeled as a state-dependent hybrid system with linear parameter varying dynamics. A compositional monitor is built by computing low-dimensional invariant sets for each region offline in a decentralized manner, then checking their conjunction online.

Result: Under common independence assumptions, the compositional monitor is sound and complete relative to the full-system invariant. The approach is validated on a real robot persistently monitoring a labyrinth.

Conclusion: Compositional monitoring via decentralized invariant computation is a practical and formally grounded approach for runtime assurance in black-box autonomous persistent surveillance systems.

Abstract: This paper studies runtime monitoring for persistent surveillance by autonomous robots when the autonomy stack is a black box. The environment is partitioned into finitely many parts, each carrying an uncertainty state that decreases when observed and increases otherwise. We model the closed loop as a state-dependent hybrid system with linear parameter varying dynamics and design a monitor based on an invariant computed offline. As this invariant is typically hard to obtain for large to-be-surveyed spaces, we propose a compositional monitor obtained by decentralized computation of low-dimensional invariant sets for each uncertainty region, and checking their conjunction online. Under common independence assumptions, the compositional monitor is sound and complete with respect to the full-system invariant. The approach is applied in a case study with a real robot persistently monitoring a labyrinth, emphasizing its applicability in practice.

</details>


### [20] [VLA-GSE: Boosting Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning in VLA with Generalized and Specialized Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06175)
*Yuhua Jiang,Junjie Lu,Xinyao Qin,Xiaoyu Chen,Kaixin Wang,Feifei Gao,Li Zhao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VLA-GSE is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning framework for VLA models that uses spectral decomposition to create generalized and specialized experts, achieving superior robotic control adaptation with only 2.51% parameter updates while preserving pre-trained knowledge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full fine-tuning of VLA models overfits on downstream robotic data and causes catastrophic forgetting of pre-trained vision-language capabilities, while existing PEFT methods struggle to adapt effectively to robot control tasks.

Method: VLA-GSE initializes by spectrally decomposing the frozen backbone: leading singular components are assigned to generalized experts (shared) and disjoint residual components to specialized experts (routed), improving adaptation under a fixed trainable-parameter budget.

Result: With only 2.51% parameter update, VLA-GSE achieves 81.2% average zero-shot success on LIBERO-Plus, preserves VLM capability comparably to LoRA, and improves real-world manipulation success under multiple distribution shifts, outperforming both FFT and PEFT baselines.

Conclusion: VLA-GSE effectively bridges the gap between knowledge preservation and control adaptation for VLA fine-tuning through spectral decomposition-based expert routing.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models inherit rich visual-semantic priors from pre-trained vision-language backbones, but adapting them to robotic control remains challenging. Full fine-tuning (FFT) is prone to overfitting on downstream robotic data and catastrophic forgetting of pretrained vision-language capabilities. Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) better preserves pre-trained knowledge, yet existing PEFT methods still struggle to adapt effectively to robot control tasks. To address this gap, we propose VLA-GSE, a parameter-efficient VLA fine-tuning framework that improves control adaptation while retaining PEFT's knowledge preservation advantage. Specifically, VLA-GSE (Generalized and Specialized Experts) is initialized by spectrally decomposing the frozen backbone, assigning leading singular components to generalized experts (shared experts) and disjoint residual components to specialized experts (routed experts). This decomposition improves adaptation capacity under a fixed trainable-parameter budget. Under a comparable parameter budget, VLA-GSE updates only 2.51% of the full model parameters and consistently outperforms strong FFT and PEFT baselines. It achieves 81.2% average zero-shot success on LIBERO-Plus, preserves pre-trained VLM capability comparably to LoRA on multimodal understanding benchmarks, and improves real-world manipulation success under multiple distribution shifts. Code is available at: https://github.com/YuhuaJiang2002/VLA-GSE

</details>


### [21] [When to Trust Imagination: Adaptive Action Execution for World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06222)
*Rui Wang,Yue Zhang,Jiehong Lin,Kuncheng Luo,Jianan Wang,Zhongrui Wang,Xiaojuan Qi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: FFDC is a lightweight verifier for World Action Models that adaptively controls action execution chunk sizes by checking consistency between predicted future and real observations, reducing compute by 69% and improving success rates by up to 35%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current WAMs execute a fixed number of predicted actions after each inference, leaving the robot blind to whether the imagined future matches reality; this causes inefficiency and failure when deviations occur.

Method: Proposes FFDC (Future Forward Dynamics Causal Attention), a lightweight verifier that jointly reasons over predicted actions, predicted visual dynamics, real observations, and language instructions to estimate whether remaining action rollout can be trusted. Also introduces Mixture-of-Horizon Training for better long-horizon coverage.

Result: On RoboTwin: 69.10% fewer WAM forward passes, 34.02% faster execution, 2.54% higher success rate vs short-chunk baseline. In real world: 35% success rate improvement.

Conclusion: Adaptive action chunk sizes via future-reality verification achieve a strong robustness-efficiency trade-off, preserving long-horizon efficiency while restoring responsiveness in difficult phases.

Abstract: World Action Models (WAMs) have recently emerged as a promising paradigm for robotic manipulation by jointly predicting future visual observations and future actions. However, current WAMs typically execute a fixed number of predicted actions after each model inference, leaving the robot blind to whether the imagined future remains consistent with the actual physical rollout. In this work, we formulate adaptive WAM execution as a future-reality verification problem: the robot should execute longer when the WAM-predicted future remains reliable, and replan earlier when reality deviates from imagination. To this end, we propose Future Forward Dynamics Causal Attention (FFDC), a lightweight verifier that jointly reasons over predicted future actions, predicted visual dynamics, real observations, and language instructions to estimate whether the remaining action rollout can still be trusted. FFDC enables adaptive action chunk sizes as an emergent consequence of prediction-observation consistency, preserving the efficiency of long-horizon execution while restoring responsiveness in contact-rich or difficult phases. We further introduce Mixture-of-Horizon Training to improve long-horizon trajectory coverage for adaptive execution. Experiments on the RoboTwin benchmark and in the real world demonstrate that our method achieves a strong robustness-efficiency trade-off: on RoboTwin, it reduces WAM forward passes by 69.10% and execution time by 34.02%, while improving success rate by 2.54% over the short-chunk baseline; in real-world experiments, it improves success rate by 35%.

</details>


### [22] [RobotEQ: Transitioning from Passive Intelligence to Active Intelligence in Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06234)
*Kuofei Fang,Xinyi Che,Haomin Ouyang,Shufan Zhang,Xuehao Wang,Qi Liu,Liyi Liu,Chenqi Zhang,Wenxi Cai,Wenyu Dai,Jinyang Wu,Fan Zhang,Haoyu Chen,Bin He,Zheng Lian*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RobotEQ introduces the first benchmark for active intelligence in Embodied AI, evaluating whether models can understand and adhere to social norms without explicit instructions, using 1,900 egocentric images, 5,353 action judgment questions, and 1,286 spatial grounding questions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Embodied AI research focuses on completing tasks based on explicit user instructions (passive intelligence). For robots to integrate into human society, they need to understand social norms—what actions are permissible or prohibited—even without explicit commands. This gap motivates the need for active intelligence benchmarks.

Method: The authors construct RobotEQ-Data (1,900 egocentric images across 10 categories and 56 subcategories) with 5,353 action judgment questions and 1,286 spatial grounding questions via extensive manual annotation. They establish RobotEQ-Bench to evaluate state-of-the-art models on active intelligence tasks, including experiments with RAG techniques incorporating external social norm knowledge bases.

Result: Current models fail to achieve reliable active intelligence, especially in spatial grounding. RAG techniques with external social norm knowledge bases generally improve performance, but overall results indicate existing models are insufficient for active social compliance.

Conclusion: RobotEQ provides the first benchmark for active intelligence, highlighting significant gaps in current models' ability to comprehend social norms. This work aims to facilitate the transition from user-guided passive manipulation to active social compliance in robotics.

Abstract: Embodied AI is a prominent research topic in both academia and industry. Current research centers on completing tasks based on explicit user instructions. However, for robots to integrate into human society, they must understand which actions are permissible and which are prohibited, even without explicit commands. We refer to the user-guided AI as passive intelligence and the unguided AI as active intelligence. This paper introduces RobotEQ, the first benchmark for active intelligence, aiming to assess whether existing models can comprehend and adhere to social norms in embodied scenarios. First, we construct RobotEQ-Data, a dataset consisting of 1,900 egocentric images, spanning 10 representative embodied categories and 56 subcategories. Through extensive manual annotation, we provide 5,353 action judgment questions and 1,286 spatial grounding questions, specifying appropriate robot actions across diverse scenarios. Furthermore, we establish RobotEQ-Bench to evaluate the performance of state-of-the-art models on this task. Experimental results show that current models still fall short in achieving reliable active intelligence, particularly in spatial grounding. Meanwhile, we observe that leveraging RAG techniques to incorporate external social norm knowledge bases can generally enhance performance. This work can facilitate the transition of robotics from user-guided passive manipulation to active social compliance.

</details>


### [23] [CKT-WAM: Parameter-Efficient Context Knowledge Transfer Between World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06247)
*Yuhua Jiang,Yijun Guo,Hongbing Yang,Guojun Lei,Nuo Chen,Yinuo Zhang,Shaoqiang Yan,Bo Lin,Feifei Gao,Biqing Qi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CKT-WAM is a parameter-efficient framework for transferring knowledge across heterogeneous world action models by injecting a compact context into the text embedding space, achieving high performance with minimal trainable parameters (1.17%) in both simulation and real-world robotic tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transferring knowledge across heterogeneous World Action Models (WAMs) is challenging due to mismatched latent interfaces, high adaptation costs, and rigidity of conventional distillation objectives.

Method: CKT-WAM extracts intermediate teacher hidden states, reduces tokens via compressors' learnable-query cross attention (LQCA), transforms them through an always-on generalized adapter, a lightweight router, and sparsely activated specialized adapters. The resulting context is appended to the student's conditioning textual embeddings.

Result: Achieves 86.1% total success rate on LIBERO-Plus with only 1.17% trainable parameters, approaching full fine-tuning performance. In real-world long-horizon tasks, achieves 83.3% average success rate across four multi-step tasks.

Conclusion: CKT-WAM consistently improves zero-shot generalization and demonstrates strong real-world long-horizon manipulation ability with minimal architectural modification and parameter overhead.

Abstract: World action models (WAMs) provide a powerful generative framework for embodied control, yet transferring knowledge across heterogeneous WAMs remains challenging due to mismatched latent interfaces, high adaptation cost, and the rigidity of conventional distillation objectives. We propose \textbf{CKT-WAM}, a parameter-efficient \textbf{C}ontext \textbf{K}nowledge \textbf{T}ransfer framework that transfers teacher WAM's knowledge into a student WAM through a compact context in the text embedding space, rather than output imitation or dense hidden-state matching. Specifically, CKT-WAM extracts intermediate teacher hidden states, reduces the number of tokens via compressors' learnable-query cross attention (LQCA), and transforms them through an always-on generalized adapter, a lightweight router, and sparsely activated specialized adapters. The resulting context is then appended to the student's conditioning textual embeddings, thereby injecting the transferred knowledge into the student with minimal architectural modification. Experiments show that CKT-WAM consistently improves zero-shot generalization and achieves the best overall performance on LIBERO-Plus, reaching 86.1\% total success rate with only 1.17\% trainable parameters, while approaching full fine-tuning performance. Beyond simulation, CKT-WAM also demonstrates strong real-world long-horizon manipulation ability, achieving the best average success rate of 83.3\% across four multi-step and long-horizon tasks. Code is available at https://github.com/YuhuaJiang2002/CKT-WAM.

</details>


### [24] [Toward Visually Realistic Simulation: A Benchmark for Evaluating Robot Manipulation in Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06311)
*Yixin Zhu,Zixiong Wang,Jian Yang,Jin Xie,Jingyi Yu,Jiayuan Gu,Beibei Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VISER is a visually realistic simulation benchmark for robot manipulation evaluation, featuring over 1,000 high-fidelity 3D assets with PBR materials. It uses an automated MLLM-based pipeline for material-aware segmentation and retrieval, and achieves a 0.92 Pearson correlation with real-world performance across diverse tasks like grasping, placing, and long-horizon manipulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing robot manipulation benchmarks lack visual realism, creating a significant sim-to-real domain gap that undermines the reliability of simulation-based evaluation for predicting real-world performance. The roles of lighting and material in geometric reasoning and spatial grounding are largely overlooked.

Method: (1) Systematic analysis isolating effects of lighting and material on sim-to-real transfer. (2) Construction of a high-fidelity dataset with over 1,000 3D assets using physically-based rendering (PBR) materials and curated/generated 3D scenes. (3) An automated pipeline using Multi-modal Large Language Models (MLLMs) for material-aware part segmentation and material retrieval. (4) Diverse evaluation tasks (grasping, placing, long-horizon) for assessing Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models.

Result: VISER achieves a strong correlation between simulation and real-world performance, with an average Pearson correlation coefficient of 0.92 across different policies.

Conclusion: VISER provides a more reliable simulation-based proxy for real-world robot manipulation by bridging the visual realism gap, enabling scalable and reproducible evaluation of VLA models.

Abstract: Reliable simulation evaluation of robot manipulation policies serves as a high-fidelity proxy for real-world performance. Although existing benchmarks cover a wide range of task categories, they lack visual realism, creating a large domain gap between simulation and reality. This undermines the reliability of simulation-based evaluation in predicting real-world performance. To mitigate the sim-to-real visual gap, we conduct a systematic analysis to isolate the effects of lighting and material. Our results show that these factors play a critical role in geometric reasoning and spatial grounding, yet are largely overlooked in existing benchmarks. Motivated by the analysis, we propose VISER, a visually realistic benchmark for evaluating robot manipulation in simulation. VISER features a high-fidelity dataset of over 1,000 3D assets with physically-based rendering (PBR) materials, along with 3D scenes created from these assets through curated layouts or generation. To this end, we propose an automated pipeline leveraging Multi-modal Large Language Models (MLLMs) for material-aware part segmentation and material retrieval, enabling scalable generation of physically plausible assets. Building on the high-fidelity 3D asset dataset, we construct diverse evaluation tasks, such as grasping, placing, and long-horizon tasks, enabling scalable and reproducible assessment of Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models. Our benchmark shows a strong correlation between simulation and real-world performance, achieving an average Pearson correlation coefficient of 0.92 across different policies.

</details>


### [25] [AssistDLO: Assistive Teleoperation for Deformable Linear Object Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06323)
*Berk Guler,Simon Manschitz,Kay Pompetzki,Jan Peters*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: AssistDLO is an assistive teleoperation framework for deformable linear object (DLO) manipulation that combines multi-view state estimation, visual assistance, and a geometry-aware shared-autonomy controller (SA-CBF). A bimanual knot-untangling user study (N=22) reveals that SA-CBF helps naive users (success rate from 71% to 88%) but experts prefer visual assistance, and effectiveness depends on DLO properties (rigidity, length), highlighting the need for adaptive, user-aware assistance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manipulating DLOs in teleoperation is difficult due to infinite-dimensional state space and depth uncertainty; existing shared autonomy methods use simple geometric attractors that fail to preserve DLO geometry.

Method: AssistDLO integrates real-time multi-view state estimation, visual assistance (VA), and a geometry-aware shared-autonomy controller based on Control Barrier Functions (SA-CBF) that acts as a geometry-preserving funnel for precise grasping while maintaining operator authority.

Result: In a user study (N=22), SA-CBF increased naive users' task success from 71% to 88%, was effective for stiffer ropes; expert users preferred VA; highly compliant long ropes benefited more from visual support than localized action assistance.

Conclusion: Effective DLO teleoperation cannot rely on a fixed strategy; adaptive, user-aware, and material-aware shared autonomy is critical.

Abstract: Manipulating Deformable Linear Objects (DLOs) is challenging in robotics due to their infinite-dimensional configuration space and complex nonlinear dynamics. In teleoperation, depth uncertainty hinders state perception and reaction. AssistDLO addresses this challenge as an assistive teleoperation framework for DLO manipulation that combines real-time multi-view state estimation, visual assistance (VA), and a geometry-aware shared-autonomy controller based on Control Barrier Functions (SA-CBF). While traditional shared autonomy methods often rely on simple geometric attractors and may fail to preserve DLO geometry, SA-CBF acts as a geometry-aware funnel, facilitating precise grasping while preserving the operator's high-level authority. The framework is evaluated in a bimanual knot-untangling user study (N = 22) using ropes with varying length and rigidity. Results show that the effectiveness of the assistance depends strongly on operator expertise and DLO properties. SA-CBF provides the strongest gains for naive users, acting as a skill equalizer that increases task success from 71% to 88%, and is effective for stiffer ropes. Conversely, expert users prefer VA, and highly compliant, long ropes benefit more from visual support than localized action assistance. Ultimately, these findings demonstrate that effective DLO teleoperation cannot rely on a fixed strategy, highlighting the critical need for adaptive, user-aware, and material-aware shared autonomy.

</details>


### [26] [TouchDrive: Electronics-Free Tactile Sensing Interface for Assistive Grasping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06432)
*Jing Xu,Xuezhi Niu,Didem Gurdur Broo,Klas Hjort*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TouchDrive is a cost-effective, electronics-free tactile sensing interface that converts contact forces into pneumatic feedback for assistive robotic grasping, validated across diverse platforms and up to 20 everyday objects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing assistive grasping systems rely on electronic sensing and multi-stage processing, increasing complexity and reducing accessibility. There is a need for a simpler, more accessible solution.

Method: TouchDrive uses a pneumatic normally closed valve, compressed air tank, sensing element, and haptic feedback actuator in a passive mechanical loop, directly converting contact forces into pneumatic feedback without electronics.

Result: Validated across diverse robotic platforms, demonstrating reliable performance in assistive grasping tasks including handling up to 20 compliant and fragile objects (fruits and everyday items).

Conclusion: TouchDrive enables precise and robust delicate manipulation of compliant and fragile objects through tactile cue-based grasp force modulation, offering a practical, low-complexity alternative to electronic sensing systems.

Abstract: Assistive robotic grasping plays an important role in enabling safe and adaptive manipulation of diverse objects. However, existing systems often rely on electronic sensing and multi-stage processing pipelines, increasing system complexity and reducing accessibility. To address these limitations, we present TouchDrive, a cost-effective, electronics-free tactile sensing interface for assistive grasping. TouchDrive directly converts contact forces into pneumatic feedback through valve-mediated switching, integrating sensing, signal generation, and feedback within a single passive mechanical loop. The system can be employed using a pneumatic normally closed valve, a compressed air tank, sensing element, and haptic feedback actuator without electronics. By delivering tactile cues, TouchDrive empowers users to modulate grasp forces, enabling precise and robust delicate manipulation of compliant and fragile objects. The interface has been validated across diverse robotic platforms, consistently demonstrating reliable performance and practical applicability in assistive grasping tasks, such as handling fruits and everyday items (up to 20 objects).

</details>


### [27] [GA3T: A Ground-Aerial Terrain Traversability Dataset for Heterogeneous Robot Teams in Unstructured Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06478)
*Siwei Cai,Knut Peterson,Quan Tran,Christian Ricks,Dhanush Parthasarathy,Amir Kaidarov,Neil Deshpande,Sukaina Najm,David Han,Lifeng Zhou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: GA3T is a real-world multi-robot collaborative perception dataset featuring a ground UGV and aerial UAV operating in diverse unstructured terrains, providing over 13,000 synchronized multi-modal frames with cross-view and cross-modal observations, designed to advance research in air-ground collaborative perception for off-road environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Progress in multi-robot collaborative perception is hindered by the lack of real-world datasets with overlapping multi-modal observations from platforms operating in unstructured terrain.

Method: Collected data using a Clearpath Husky UGV (3D LiDAR, stereo camera, IMU, GPS) and Autel EVO II UAV (RGB, thermal/IR, GPS) across 4 environments (forest trails, rocky paths, muddy terrain, snow piles, grass fields), with over 13,000 synchronized frames (~29 min), SAM 3-based zero-shot segmentation, and over 8,000 manual labels; early-spring collection enables occlusion-aware perception through sparse tree canopies.

Result: A real-world dataset with 13,000+ synchronized frames across 4 unstructured environments, featuring multi-modal, cross-view, and occlusion-aware collaborative perception data from air-ground robot teams.

Conclusion: GA3T uniquely supports cross-view perception, air-ground viewpoint fusion, traversability estimation, and collaborative scene understanding in real off-road environments, filling a gap left by prior datasets focused on SLAM or simulated cooperative driving.

Abstract: Heterogeneous air-ground robot teams combine complementary sensing modalities, mobility characteristics, and spatial viewpoints that can significantly enhance perception in complex outdoor environments. However, progress in multi-robot collaborative perception has been constrained by the lack of real-world datasets featuring overlapping multi-modal observations from platforms operating in unstructured terrain. We present GA3T (Ground-Aerial Team for Terrain Traversal), a real-world multi-robot collaborative perception dataset collected using a Clearpath Husky UGV and an Autel EVO~II UAV across diverse unstructured environments, including forest trails, rocky paths, muddy terrain, snow piles, and grass-covered fields.
